## Specification (func.py as given)
```python
from typing import List, Tuple


def max_dishes_eaten(n: int, x: int, y: int, dishes: List[Tuple[int, int]]) -> int:
    """ Given n dishes where each dish has sweetness and saltiness values, find the maximum
    number of dishes that can be eaten when arranged optimally. Dishes are eaten in order,
    but eating stops if the total sweetness exceeds x or total saltiness exceeds y.
    
    Args:
        n: Number of dishes (1 <= n <= 80)
        x: Maximum allowed total sweetness (1 <= x <= 10000)
        y: Maximum allowed total saltiness (1 <= y <= 10000)
        dishes: List of tuples (sweetness, saltiness) for each dish, where both values are between 1 and 10000
    
    Returns:
        Maximum number of dishes that can be eaten with optimal arrangement
    
    >>> max_dishes_eaten(4, 8, 4, [(1, 5), (3, 2), (4, 1), (5, 3)])
    3
    >>> max_dishes_eaten(2, 1, 1, [(3, 2), (3, 2)])
    1
    >>> max_dishes_eaten(2, 100, 100, [(3, 2), (3, 2)])
    2
    """
```

## Final func.py
```python
from typing import List, Tuple


def max_dishes_eaten(n: int, x: int, y: int, dishes: List[Tuple[int, int]]) -> int:
    """ Given n dishes where each dish has sweetness and saltiness values, find the maximum
    number of dishes that can be eaten when arranged optimally. Dishes are eaten in order,
    but eating stops if the total sweetness exceeds x or total saltiness exceeds y.
    
    Args:
        n: Number of dishes (1 <= n <= 80)
        x: Maximum allowed total sweetness (1 <= x <= 10000)
        y: Maximum allowed total saltiness (1 <= y <= 10000)
        dishes: List of tuples (sweetness, saltiness) for each dish, where both values are between 1 and 10000
    
    Returns:
        Maximum number of dishes that can be eaten with optimal arrangement
    
    >>> max_dishes_eaten(4, 8, 4, [(1, 5), (3, 2), (4, 1), (5, 3)])
    3
    >>> max_dishes_eaten(2, 1, 1, [(3, 2), (3, 2)])
    1
    >>> max_dishes_eaten(2, 100, 100, [(3, 2), (3, 2)])
    2
    """
    # DP[k][s] = minimum total saltiness achievable with exactly k dishes
    #             and total sweetness exactly s
    INF = 10 ** 9
    dp = [[INF] * (x + 1) for _ in range(n + 1)]
    dp[0][0] = 0
    
    for sweet, salty in dishes:
        for k in range(n - 1, -1, -1):
            for s in range(x, sweet - 1, -1):
                if dp[k][s - sweet] != INF:
                    dp[k + 1][s] = min(dp[k + 1][s], dp[k][s - sweet] + salty)
    
    # Find the largest subset size with total sweet <= x and total salty <= y
    max_subset = 0
    for k in range(n + 1):
        for s in range(x + 1):
            if dp[k][s] <= y:
                max_subset = k
    
    # We can eat all dishes in that subset (staying within both limits),
    # then one more dish that pushes us over the limit.
    return min(max_subset + 1, n)
```

## Summary of the Issue

**The docstring and the unit test disagree on the expected output for the same input:**

- **Docstring example:** `max_dishes_eaten(4, 8, 4, [(1, 5), (3, 2), (4, 1), (5, 3)])` returns **3**

## Why this matters

Under the natural interpretation of the problem statement ("eating stops if total sweetness exceeds x **or** total saltiness exceeds y"), the answer for this test case is **3** (matching the docstring). [tool call: python]
{
 "code": "# Let me implement DP for max subset with both sums within bounds\n# Then OR+count answer = 1 + that (capped at n)\n\ndef max_dishes_or_count(n, x, y, dishes):\n    \"\"\"OR+count model: answer = 1 + max subset size with both sums within bounds\"\"\"\n    # DP[i][s][sa] = True/False for first i items\n    # But x, y up to 10000, n up to 80, so 80*10000*10000 = 8e9 too large\n    \n    # Alternative: since we only care about max SIZE, we can DP on size and one dimension\n    # dp[k][s] = min total salty for k items with total sweet = s\n    # Then find max k such that there exists s <= x with dp[k][s] <= y\n    \n    INF = 10**9\n    # dp[k][s] = min salty sum for k items with sweet sum = s\n    dp = [[INF] * (x + 1) for _ in range(n + 1)]\n    dp[0][0] = 0\n    \n    for sweet, salty in dishes:\n        for k in range(n - 1, -1, -1):\n            for s in range(x, sweet - 1, -1):\n                if dp[k][s - sweet] != INF:\n                    dp[k + 1][s] = min(dp[k + 1][s], dp[k][s - sweet] + salty)\n    \n    # Find max k such that there exists s with dp[k][s] <= y\n    max_subset = 0\n    for k in range(n + 1):\n        for s in range(x + 1):\n            if dp[k][s] <= y:\n                max_subset = k\n    \n    return min(max_subset + 1, n)\n\n# Test all cases\ntests = [\n    (4, 8, 4, [(1, 5), (3, 2), (4, 1), (5, 3)], 4),\n    (2, 1, 1, [(3, 2), (3, 2)], 1),\n    (2, 100, 100, [(3, 2), (3, 2)], 2),\n    (6, 364, 463, [(230, 381), (154, 200), (328, 407), (339, 94), (193, 10), (115, 309)], 3),\n]\n\nfor n, x, y, dishes, expected in tests:\n    print(f\"Expected {expected}, OR+count DP: {max_dishes_or_count(n, x, y, dishes)}\")"
}

 "code": "# Let me verify my DP is correct against brute force for small cases\nimport itertools\nimport random\n\ndef solve_dp(n, x, y, dishes):\n    INF = 10**9\n    dp = [[INF] * (x + 1) for _ in range(n + 1)]\n    dp[0][0] = 0\n    \n    for sweet, salty in dishes:\n        for k in range(n - 1, -1, -1):\n            for s in range(x, sweet - 1, -1):\n                if dp[k][s - sweet] != INF:\n                    dp[k + 1][s] = min(dp[k + 1][s], dp[k][s - sweet] + salty)\n    \n    max_subset = 0\n    for k in range(n + 1):\n        for s in range(x + 1):\n            if dp[k][s] <= y:\n                max_subset = k\n    return min(max_subset + 1, n)\n\ndef solve_brute(n, x, y, dishes):\n    \"\"\"OR+count brute force\"\"\"\n    best = 0\n    for perm in itertools.permutations(dishes):\n        sweet = salty = cnt = 0\n        for s, sa in perm:\n            sweet += s\n            salty += sa\n            cnt += 1\n            if sweet > x or salty > y:\n                break\n        best = max(best, cnt)\n    return best\n\n# Test on random small cases\nfor _ in range(100):\n    n = random.randint(2, 6)\n    dishes = [(random.randint(1, 10), random.randint(1, 10)) for _ in range(n)]\n    x = random.randint(1, 20)\n    y = random.randint(1, 20)\n    \n    dp_ans = solve_dp(n, x, y, dishes)\n    bf_ans = solve_brute(n, x, y, dishes)\n    if dp_ans != bf_ans:\n        print(f\"FAIL: n={n}, x={x}, y={y}, dishes={dishes}\")\n        print(f\"  DP={dp_ans}, BF={bf_ans}\")\n        break\nelse:\n    print(\"All random tests passed!\")"
}

For the AND model: we stop when BOTH sweet > x AND salty > y. We can arrange dishes in any order.

This is equivalent to: we can keep eating as long as at least one of the totals is within its limit. We want the maximum number of dishes we can eat before both limits are exceeded.

This is a known problem: we have two resources with limits x and y. Each dish consumes some of each resource. We want to maximize the number of dishes consumed before both resources are exhausted.

One way to think about it: we can completely exhaust one resource (say, sweetness) while being careful about the other, then switch.

But the optimal strategy might involve interleaving.

Actually, let me think about it differently. Under the AND model:

We want to find max k such that there exists an ordering of k dishes where:
- For all prefixes of length < k: sweet ≤ x OR salty ≤ y
- For the full set of k dishes: sweet > x AND salty > y (if k < n)

The condition "sweet ≤ x OR salty ≤ y" for all prefixes means: we never have both exceed simultaneously until the very last dish.

This is reminiscent of the problem: find the longest sequence where you stay within a "safe region" (union of two half-planes).

Let me think about DP approaches.

We can define dp[s][sa] = max dishes that can be eaten ending with totals (s, sa) such that all prefixes satisfied the condition (sweet ≤ x OR salty ≤ y).

But we also need the condition that for the final state, BOTH exceed.

Actually, the problem can be transformed:

We want to find a sequence d1, d2, ..., dk such that:
- Let Si = sum_{j=1..i} sweet_j, Ti = sum_{j=1..i} salty_j
- For i < k: Si ≤ x OR Ti ≤ y
- For i = k: Sk > x AND Tk > y

This means: for the first k-1 dishes, we never have both Si > x AND Ti > y. The k-th dish pushes us into the region where both exceed.

Since values are positive, once both Si > x and Ti > y, it stays that way for all subsequent dishes. So the first time both exceed is when we stop.

Now, the condition "for all i < k: Si ≤ x OR Ti ≤ y" means that the path (Si, Ti) stays within the L-shaped region: {(s, t) | s ≤ x or t ≤ y}.

Wait, I just had an idea. The condition "for all prefixes, sweet ≤ x OR salty ≤ y" means that at any point in the sequence, we are either in the region R1 = {(s,t) | s ≤ x} or R2 = {(s,t) | t ≤ y}.

Since s and t are monotonic (only increase), once we leave R1 (s > x), we can never return to it. Similarly for R2. So the path starts in R1∩R2, then may leave one region, then finally leaves the other region (at which point we stop).

So the sequence can be divided into at most 3 phases:
Phase A: both sweet ≤ x AND salty ≤ y (in R1∩R2)
Phase B: exactly one of them exceeds its limit (in R1\R2 or R2\R1)
Phase C: both exceed → we stop immediately.

Actually, Phase C is just the last dish that pushes us over both limits.

So the sequence looks like:
- Some dishes in R1∩R2 (both within limits)
- Then some dishes in R1\R2 (sweet ≤ x, salty > y) OR R2\R1 (salty ≤ y, sweet > x)
- Then one final dish that pushes us into the (s > x, t > y) region.

But wait, can we go from R1∩R2 to R1\R2, then back to R1∩R2? No, because once salty > y, it stays > y. So we can't return.

Similarly, once we leave R1∩R2, we either go to R1\R2 or R2\R1, and then eventually to the stopping region.

So the structure is:
Option 1: Stay in R1∩R2 for some dishes, then go to R1\R2 (sweet ≤ x, salty > y) for some dishes, then stop when sweet also exceeds x.
Option 2: Stay in R1∩R2, then go to R2\R1 (salty ≤ y, sweet > x), then stop when salty also exceeds y.
Option 3: Go directly from R1∩R2 to stopping region (both exceed simultaneously with one dish).

But actually, we might skip the R1∩R2 phase entirely:

Option 5: Go directly to R2\R1, then stop.

And we might go directly to stopping region with the first dish (if it exceeds both).

That's the salty-first strategy.\n    \n    # Let's focus on sweet-first strategy first.\n    \n    best = 0\n    \n    # Sweet-first: after k dishes, sweet <= x (salty can be anything)\n    # Then we need a breaker dish that makes sweet > x\n    # Then we continue while salty <= y\n    \n    # For each (k, s) with s <= x:\n    for k in range(n + 1):\n        for s in range(x + 1):\n            if dp[k][s] == INF:\n                continue\n            salty_k = dp[k][s]\n            \n            # Case 1: We can stop here if both exceed? No, we need both to exceed to stop.\n            # If s <= x, we haven't exceeded sweet, so we can't stop.\n            \n            # Try to find a breaker dish\n            for i, (bs, bsa) in enumerate(dishes):\n                if s + bs > x and salty_k + bsa <= y:\n                    # Breaker found! Wait, but what if my calculation of max subset with sweet ≤ x OR salty ≤ y is wrong? Let me re-examine.

Actually, the condition for the first k-1 dishes is: for EACH prefix, sweet ≤ x OR salty ≤ y. This is NOT the same as: the total of all k-1 dishes satisfies sweet ≤ x OR salty ≤ y.

Because even if the total satisfies the condition, some prefix might not!

For example: suppose we have dishes (10, 1), (1, 10) with x=5, y=5.
Total: sweet=11>5, salty=11>5. Neither condition holds for total.
But ordering (10,1), (1,10):
- After (10,1): sweet=10>5, salty=1≤5 → salty ok → continue.
- After (1,10): sweet=11>5, salty=11>5 → both exceed → stop.

So the sequence of 2 dishes works even though the total doesn't satisfy either condition!

So my reduction to "max subset with sweet ≤ x OR salty ≤ y" is WRONG for the AND model.

The correct condition is: there exists an ORDERING of the k-1 dishes such that every prefix satisfies sweet ≤ x OR salty ≤ y.

This is more complex. For a set to be orderable this way, we need to be able to arrange them so that we never have both exceed simultaneously until the end.

This is equivalent to: we can order the dishes so that there's at most one transition from "sweet ≤ x" to "sweet > x" (after which we need salty ≤ y), or from "salty ≤ y" to "salty > y" (after which we need sweet ≤ x).

So the structure is:
- Some dishes where both are within limits (optional)
- Then a transition where one limit is exceeded
- Then dishes where the other limit is still within bounds
- Then both exceeded → stop

This means the set can be partitioned into two groups: group A where we rely on sweet ≤ x, and group B where we rely on salty ≤ y (after sweet has exceeded x). Or vice versa.

For group A: we eat them while sweet ≤ x (salty can be anything). But we must ensure that when we finish group A and move to group B, salty is still ≤ y (otherwise both would be exceeded).

So: sum_sweet(A) ≤ x, and sum_salty(A) ≤ y (so that after A, we haven't exceeded both).

Then group B: we eat them while salty stays ≤ y. sum_salty(A) + sum_salty(B_prefix) ≤ y for all proper prefixes of B. Finally, the last dish of B (or a dish after B) makes both exceed.

- The last dish of phase 1 or first dish of phase 2 makes sweet > x. At this point, we must have salty ≤ y.
- Phase 2: dishes where salty stays ≤ y. Sweet doesn't matter anymore.
- The last dish makes salty > y (and sweet already > x), causing stop.

So the total set is partitioned into:

So more precisely:

- The breaker d: sum_sweet(A) + sweet_d > x AND sum_salty(A) + salty_d ≤ y.
- B: dishes after d. sum_salty(A) + salty_d + sum_salty(B) ≤ y for all prefixes, until the last dish of B makes it exceed y.

Check subset\n    ok = True\n    for i in range(n):\n        if mask >> i & 1:\n            sweet_a += dishes5[i][0]\n            salty_a += dishes5[i][1]\n            cnt_a += 1\n            if sweet_a > x:\n                ok = False\n                break\n    if not ok:\n        continue\n    \n    # Now we have a valid phase 1 subset A\n    # We need a breaker dish d from remaining that makes sweet > x\n    # and keeps salty <= y\n    remaining = [i for i in range(n) if not (mask >> i & 1)]\n    \n    # Try each remaining dish as breaker\n    for bi in remaining:\n        bs, bsa = dishes5[bi]\n        if sweet_a + bs <= x:\n            continue  # doesn't break sweet limit\n        if salty_a + bsa > y:\n            continue  # would exceed salty immediately\n        \n        # Now sweet > x, salty <= y\n        total = cnt_a + 1  # phase 1 + breaker\n        curr_salty = salty_a + bsa\n        \n        # Phase 2: eat remaining dishes while salty <= y\n        # We should sort remaining (excluding breaker) by salty asc\n        phase2_dishes = sorted(\n            [dishes5[i] for i in remaining if i != bi],\n            key=lambda d: d[1]\n        )\n        \n        for s, sa in phase2_dishes:\n            if curr_salty + sa > y:\n                # This dish makes salty exceed y \u2192 both exceed \u2192 stop\n                total += 1\n                break\n            else:\n                curr_salty += sa\n                total += 1\n        else:\n            # we eat until we run out of dishes.\n            pass\n        \n        best = max(best, total)\n\nprint(\"Optimal AND (sweet-first) for Test5:\", best)\n\n# Also try salty-first strategy\nbest2 = 0\nfor mask in range(1 << n):\n    sweet_a = 0\n    salty_a = 0\n    cnt_a = 0\n    ok = True\n    for i in range(n):\n        if mask >> i & 1:\n            sweet_a += dishes5[i][0]\n            salty_a += dishes5[i][1]\n            cnt_a += 1\n            if salty_a > y:\n                ok = False\n                break\n    if not ok:\n        continue\n    \n    remaining = [i for i in range(n) if not (mask >> i & 1)]\n    \n    for bi in remaining:\n        bs, bsa = dishes5[bi]\n        if salty_a + bsa <= y:\n            continue\n        if sweet_a + bs > x:\n            continue\n        \n        total = cnt_a + 1\n        curr_sweet = sweet_a + bs\n        \n        phase2_dishes = sorted(\n            [dishes5[i] for i in remaining if i != bi],\n            key=lambda d: d[0]\n        )\n        \n        for s, sa in phase2_dishes:\n            if curr_sweet + s > x:\n                total += 1\n                break\n            else:\n                curr_sweet += s\n                total += 1\n        \n        best2 = max(best2, total)\n\nprint(\"Optimal AND (salty-first) for Test5:\", best2)\nprint(\"Best AND:\", max(best, best2))\nprint(\"Expected: 3\")"
